DM: Out of the story first, because I owe you a correction. I said Thornhallow was a day's ride from the causeway. It isn't. Saltmarsh to Marrowford is four days by the river road, and Thornhallow lies a day below Marrowford, on the way. From your camp it is about two more days by wagon. You'll reach it on the afternoon of Day 18.

Now the story. You get the column moving at first light, with Kit scouting ahead and you riding by the wagon so you can watch both prisoners. Before you leave you take Pell aside, and he nods at every word. Two riders go a mile ahead and two a mile behind, and a third goes back to Saltmarsh with your warning for the arbiter: the Heron's Tithe and Sparrow are both still loose, and Cobb Harrow may come for his coin.

The road runs inland across low farmland, with the river glinting to the north. You hum the road song, and by noon the guards are humming it back. You watch the horizon the whole way, and your Perception comes up an 11 only, enough to see rooks and a hay-cart and nothing else. Ansel rides in silence on the bench beside Corvin. Neither of them has spoken since dawn. At one halt you catch Ansel looking at your lamp-staff, and when you meet his eye he looks away.

At dusk the column stops at a wayside farm with a barn and a well, and the farmer, who knows Pell's grey cloak, gives you the barn without asking questions. It's seven in the evening on Day 16. The outriders report quiet roads, and one of the rear guards says he saw a grey sail far out on the estuary at midday, too far off to count for anything. You have 17 of 17 hit points, all three slots, three Bardic Inspirations, and Kit is at full strength. Two prisoners and three bandits are bedded down in the barn. What does Wren do?

DM: You carry a lantern into the barn after supper and set it on an upturned bucket. Corvin sits against a post with his hands bound in front of him, and Pell leans in the doorway where he can see both of you and hear nothing. Ansel is at the far end of the barn, out of earshot, with his eyes closed.

You ask it straight. "Twenty years you knew the staff was at the Wick-House and never dared take it. Why not ask Ansel for help, or sell him the lamp yourself? And is his story about being her patron true?"

Corvin answers the second half first, in an easy tone. "The patron is true, child. I saw him once at Elowen's table, a younger Verrel with a ring on his hand. She wore a mark of the same lantern on a chain, and I knew it for her promise to him." He rubs his bound wrists on his knee. "As for dare, Mother Ysolde would have taken the skin off me, and I'm fond of mine."

Your Insight check is a 17, and his Deception is a 13. You can see the seam as plainly as a torn page. His voice stays smooth, but his eyes drop to the lantern flame for half a breath, the way they used to when he trimmed a story.

"That's a half-lie, Corvin," you say quietly. "Which half?"

He studies you a long moment. Then his shoulders drop, just a little. "All right," he says. "Twelve years ago a go-between named H. came to my wagon with coin from a man with a green ring. He wanted me to watch a girl with Elowen's face, and the staff she might one day claim. I sent two vague letters. I kept the coin four years." He looks at his hands. "Then I stopped answering and took up the ledger instead, because it paid better and asked fewer questions. I never gave him your name or the town. I couldn't. I didn't know it myself, then." A dry breath that isn't a laugh. "You wanted to know why I never sold him the lamp. He would never have paid for it. He wanted it for nothing, and he wanted you with it."

It is nearly half past seven. The lantern gutters, Pell hasn't moved, and across the barn Ansel hasn't opened his eyes. You now know Hale was Ansel's go-between and that Corvin took Ansel's coin for four years. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots, three Inspirations, and an unanswered question about how much Ansel has been lying to you. What does Wren do?

DM: You stand, give Ansel a flat look, and say, "Then you can tell it to Dame Crowe under oath, and I'll be sitting where you can see me." You hand the lantern to Pell and tell him to keep the two at opposite ends of the barn overnight. He nods, and you walk out into the cold, clean air of the yard.

You sit on the well-rim with the lamp-staff across your knees, and Kit sits down beside you. She doesn't say anything for a while. Then you tell her quietly, "I can't tell which of those two is lying. I'm going to write every word down tonight, who said what and when, so a court can weigh it when I can't." She considers this. "A court'll believe a page before it believes a girl," she says dryly. "Write fair. If you leave out a word that helps him, they'll find it." You glance at her, and she shrugs. "I was a village spearwoman. We kept the toll-book."

So you write. It takes an hour by the farmhouse window. You put down the barn conversations in your small, careful hand, with times and places and every word you can remember, including the ones you wish weren't there. Corvin's four years of coin, H. as the go-between, Ansel's "patron" story, his "I did not give the order," and your own Insight rolls, noted plain. When you finish you read it through. It's not a flattering record, and that's how you know it's honest. You blow on the page to dry the ink and slip it into your psalter's binding.

When you come out again it's nearly nine at night. Kit is on her feet at the yard gate, looking south across the dark river meadow. "Three flashes," she says, very low. "Shuttered lantern. And one answer." She points. A mile off, near the water, a pinprick of yellow answers once and goes out. It could be a ferryman, or a poacher with a dark-lantern. It could also be something worse.

You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots, three Inspirations, and a barn full of prisoners. Pell's watch hasn't seen it yet. What does Wren do?

His Insight is plain to you, a 20 against nothing at all. There's no story in it, only dread and the stubbornness of a man who has finally picked a road. "Then Crowe may do as she likes."

Beside him Corvin stares at the sky and says nothing. For once he has nothing to sell.

The afternoon is long and the road is dull. The wagon rattles through stubble fields and past a ruined mill. At seven in the evening the column reaches a roadside inn, the Plough and Pail. The doors were barred at dawn, but the churchyard is open to anyone willing to climb. She sets a bolt in her crossbow and says nothing. "My name is Ansel," he says. "I am the son of Hugh Verrel, Aldous's eldest, and of Joan, a ferryman's daughter at Dunnock. Your uncle did not drown, cousin. He was put on a southern coach at twenty because he would not give her up, and he died of a fever in a hired room before I was born. Your father cut my name from this book when I was ten, because a ferryman's grandson could not stand in the line of the Aldermere blood." His voice is low and steady. "I went by other names. I lost this one and went by Aldous, for the grandsire, because I thought I might wear it until I'd earned the other back. I haven't."

Nobody speaks. Osric's lips are moving. Edric looks at the scraped line, then at the long chin, the same chin as his own, and sits down slowly.

"What did you want?" Edric says at last. "Thornhallow?"

"I wanted to be named," says Ansel. "In the book where I was struck. I wanted the lamp that my grandsire gave and my father's brother lost, and I thought a man who held it could ask. I was wrong about all of it, and I have hurt people who didn't deserve it." He turns his head, just once, and finds your eye. "One of them is standing by your door."

The lamps hiss. Edric's hands are trembling on the desk. He doesn't answer Ansel, and he doesn't look away from him either.

He speaks for most of an hour, evenly, and you write it all down on a clerk's paper. The recommendation letter that brought him to Thornhallow came from Ferrand Oake, who keeps a counting-house in Marrowford and places clerks in half the minor houses on the river. Oake's strongroom passed the green-ringed man's coin to him. "Whose coin, I never asked, and I was never told," he says. "Aldous, we called him. Never a face." He doesn't know whether Oake knows who "Aldous" really is. His own cut, the two shillings in the pound, he kept in a tin box under a loose board in his room in the east wing, with every tally in his own hand. "Insurance, mistress. You'd have done the same."

When he finishes, he sits back. "I don't expect mercy. I do expect Dame Crowe to read the tallies." You read the pages back to him, and he nods without comment to each. Kit looks at you over his head.

DM: Out of the story first: a small correction. The Gullspit lamp lit on Day 10, not Day 7. It doesn't change your point, which is that a man who claims never to have known what "Aldous" wanted knew to write "for the lamp".

Now the story. You let Oake finish signing, then set your record of the quay events on the table and tap the line you copied from his clerk's sheet. "Master Oake, your own tally says you paid Cobb Harrow forty marks to hold the Heron's Tithe at Sedge Creek until the 24th, 'for the lamp.' A man who never saw Aldous's face doesn't know about a lamp. So who told you about it, and how did you know to hold the cutter until the 24th?" You keep your voice mild and watch his hands.

Your Insight is a 15 against his Deception of 10. He opens his mouth, closes it, and smiles. "Aldous's letters mentioned the lamp, mistress. The holding was his standing instruction. A bill drawn ahead on the twelfth." It is a good answer, and it is quick. But his hands, which have been lying so still on his knees, stop being still. His left thumb goes to the base of his ring finger, to the place where a ring would sit, and finds nothing there, and his eyes slide to the window and back. You've seen Corvin do exactly that when a story needed one more beat.

"The bill was paid on the seventeenth," you say. "Two days after Aldous was taken on the causeway." You let it lie. "That is a very long standing instruction."

He doesn't answer. For the first time the warmth goes out of his face and something older and flatter looks out. "I think," he says slowly, "I will say no more until Dame Crowe's clerk is here." Warden Lyle, who has been listening with her chin on her fist, nods at the watchman. "That will do for the morning," she says. "He sleeps on it."

You have the seam written down, with Lyle's name beside it. It is twenty to seven on Day 20. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. The chest sits under seal and the clerk comes at noon. Oake's story has a crack in it, and Dunstan Crake is still loose. What does Wren do?

DM: Out of the story first, on the handwriting. The old letter in Hale's tin box was signed "for F. Oake", which means a clerk wrote it and signed on Oake's behalf. It is not in Oake's own hand. Comparing it from memory, your Investigation is an 18. The round, looped hand on the old letter matches the clerk's hand on the Harrow tally-sheet, with the same long-tailed F and the same looped g. Oake's own hand on today's statement is upright and cramped, nothing like it. So the clerk wrote both, and the clerk has served Oake for at least twelve years.

Now the story. "Warden, Oake's bill was paid two days after Ansel was taken. Someone is still giving orders, and I'd bet it's Oake himself." Lyle looks at you, then at her keys. She lends you two watchmen and a notary from the bridge-house as witness, and she goes with you herself. Oake's maid lets you in with the clerk's keys.

The strongroom is a small oak-and-iron room behind the office, and it is bare. Your Investigation is a 21. The iron shelves have pale rectangles in the dust where boxes stood a day ago. A drift of wax-flakes lies by the door, from broken seals. In the loading-door lock there are fresh scrapes where a key was forced and not turned. In the alley mud outside runs a hand-cart rut, curving toward the east gate. In the grating of the strongroom floor, caught on a nail, you find a torn corner of a bill stub from Tern & Bellwether, the Saltmarsh bankers. "40 m., 17th, F.O. by his own hand." Oake's own hand drew that bill. The rest of the stub is gone.

The clerk, Corbin Sedley, has not been seen since the afternoon before. His stool is cold, his coat is missing, and his abacus lies on its side. Lyle sets her seal across the strongroom door in green wax. She sends two riders to the east gate to ask about a hand-cart and a lean grey-coated man. The chest, still under the bridge-house seal, holds, you suspect, a great deal of dull grain accounting.

It's twenty past nine on Day 20. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations, and 78 gp. Dame Crowe's clerk arrives at noon to open the chest. Crake and the clerk are gone with whatever was in the strongroom, and Pell's wagon will be at the gates by dusk. What does Wren do?

On the road I read the rut for depth and freshness, and ask each gate-keeper along the east road if a grey-coated man with a notched ear passed with a clerk and a hand-cart.

"Paid the toll and said they were taking grain-sacks to a mill," he says. "Turned off the river road toward Hollin Mill."

Hollin Mill is three miles east, a tall timber building beside a millpond with a stopped wheel. You halt behind a hedge a quarter-mile off and look. Your Perception is a 20, and you see more than you expected. The hand-cart stands in the mill yard with its horse out of harness. Smoke rises from the miller's chimney. A flat skiff lies tied at the sluice, loaded low, as if for carrying papers on to the river. And in the loft window above the wheel-house a man is sitting half-hidden behind the shutter with a crossbow across his knee, watching the road. The shutter is cracked just wide enough for him.

Kit takes the whole scene in without a word. Perrin has gone pale. It's half past ten on Day 20. == ROUND 178 ==
PLAYER: I drop flat behind the thickest part of the hedge to break the lookout's line, and cast Vicious Mockery at him through the gap, loud enough for the whole mill yard to hear: "Hang up your crossbow, friend, your aim is as crooked as Crake's ear!"

DM: Out of the story for a moment, because the distance matters here. You're about forty paces from the sluice. That's roughly 120 feet, and Vicious Mockery reaches 60. The loft window is by the wheel-house, so it's too far for the spell, even through a gap. Mage Hand reaches 30 feet, so the knot is farther off still.

You drop flat behind the thickest part of the hedge, and the crossbow bolt that would have found you goes over the top instead. It hisses through the thorn and snaps a twig an arm's length above your head. So the lookout shoots at hedges. Kit's crossbow is still laid on the gate rail behind you, and nobody has fought yet. The yard is full of shouting. A man is dragging the horse back toward the cart-shafts.

You can lie where you are, with the hedge between you and the loft. You can crawl the ditch toward the sluice, which takes a few moments and the lookout is watching for it. Or you can pull back to Kit and do something else.

DM: You tell Kit to shoot only if he leans out, and she answers with a flat "Aye." Then you go down the ditch on your belly, in cold water to the elbows, with the bank's lip a hand above your head. A bolt thumps into the hedge twice, well behind you. The lookout is still shooting at the place where he last saw you. Your Stealth is a 17 against his 4, and he never sees the ditch.

Thirty feet from the sluice you stop, with the weed-green timbers of the sluice-gate above you. The skiff rides low at the post, its painted gunwale near the water, and the line is tied in a hitch around an iron ring. You whisper the words, and a pale hand the colour of candle-smoke rises from the water beside the post. It plucks at the knot, picks the hitch loose and lets the rope fall. The skiff doesn't move at first. The hand is too light to push its load, which is my ruling. Then the slow draw of the sluice takes hold, and the boat slides out from the bank with its stern turning, heading for the open pond. It won't be quick, but it's going.

Voices break out in the yard. "The boat! The boat's going!" Someone runs to the water's edge with a boathook. The hand will last about a minute, and you can still see it hanging there. The mill door bangs wide, and a lean man in a grey coat comes out with a short club, Dunstan Crake, with two heavy men behind him. He looks at the skiff and the pond, then along the hedge, and he is looking for you.

It's twenty to eleven on Day 20. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. You're in a wet ditch thirty feet from the sluice, and the nearest of Crake's men is just across the yard. Kit is forty paces back at the gate, and the watch is not yet on the road. What does Wren do?
